    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "A persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\\ synthetic) decisions.\nEven when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.",
    "newString": "A persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\\ synthetic) decisions.\nRecent efforts extend detection to multi-class settings: \\citet{beyond_binary} propose role recognition and involvement measurement to distinguish collaborative human-LLM generation scenarios, and the SemEval-2024 shared task \\cite{semeval2024_task8} evaluates multi-generator attribution across domains and languages.\nThese approaches discriminate among different \\emph{sources} (human vs.\\ specific models) rather than recursive generation \\emph{depth} within a single generator's chain.\nEven when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}\n\\label{sec:related-detection}\n\nMethods for distinguishing human-written from machine-generated text have evolved from statistical tests on token-level distributions \\cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies} to zero-shot approaches exploiting reference-model likelihoods \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt} and learned classifiers over linguistic features \\cite{feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}.\nRecent work has pursued diverse detection signals: \\citet{intrinsic_dimension} use intrinsic dimensionality estimates, \\citet{diveye} propose surprisal diversity features directly comparable to our shape-based measures, and \\citet{generator_barrier} characterize the distributional gap between human and model outputs.\n\\citet{jawahar_survey} provide a comprehensive survey.\nA persistent limitation is that all these methods produce binary (real vs.\\ synthetic) decisions.\nEven when detection scores correlate with generation depth, they exhibit high cross-generator variance when repurposed as ordinal predictors (CI spanning 0.41--0.82 in macro-F1; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), because each score captures a single distributional property that generalizes inconsistently.\n\\citet{paraphrase_evades} and \\citet{detection_possibilities} further demonstrate that detection robustness degrades under distributional shift.\nOur reference-model entropy features are functionally NLL-based out-of-distribution scores \\cite{ood_baseline}; the contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the empirical finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Source attribution vs.\\ depth estimation.}\nA related but distinct task is \\emph{source attribution}: identifying \\emph{which} model generated a given text.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} train multi-class classifiers over stylometric features to distinguish outputs of different generators, and \\citet{munir_looking_glass} learn attribution models that generalize across unseen language models.\nThese methods answer \\emph{who} generated the text; depth estimation answers \\emph{how many recursive steps} produced it.\nThe two tasks are orthogonal: a single generator can produce text at any depth, and texts at the same depth may originate from different generators.\nOur LOGO evaluation explicitly decouples generator identity from depth by holding out entire generators, ensuring that the classifier learns depth signal rather than generator fingerprints.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}\n\\label{sec:related-collapse}\n\nA growing body of work studies quality degradation when models are trained on their own outputs.\n\\citet{self_consuming_mad} demonstrate that self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage, and \\citet{curse_of_recursion} show that model collapse is inevitable in successive fine-tuning rounds without fresh human data, though \\citet{model_collapse_avoidable} identify conditions under which careful data mixing can mitigate this effect.\n\\citet{strong_model_collapse} extend these results to larger-scale settings.\nThese studies focus on \\emph{training-loop} degradation, where model weights are updated on recursive data.\nOur setting is different: we study \\emph{inference chains}, where a fixed model generates text conditioned on its own prior output without retraining.\n\nTwo concurrent lines of work are most directly relevant.\n\\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, showing progressive information loss analogous to the telephone game.\n\\citet{markovian_chains} provide a theoretical framework for convergence in iterative text transformations, demonstrating that semantic-preservation constraints induce attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes.\nOur work differs in two respects: we study \\emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal beyond the attractor bound; and we focus on \\emph{estimating} depth from text features rather than characterizing convergence behavior.\n\n\\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}\n\\label{sec:related-diversity}\n\nDeclining lexical diversity in LLM-generated text is documented in both training \\cite{curious_decline} and deployment \\cite{writing_diversity} settings.\nOur surface features---type-token ratio and hapax ratio---instantiate this observation for inference chains, where we show monotonic diversity decline across generation depths without any model retraining.\n\nThese features are classical stylometric measures with a long history in authorship attribution \\cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}.\n\\citet{uchendu_attribution} extend stylometric and neural methods to multi-class attribution of text to specific generators.\nWe push beyond generator identity to the \\emph{depth} dimension: text from the same generator at different depths exhibits systematically different feature profiles, and these profiles generalize across generators.\nSampling strategies also affect diversity; \\citet{nucleus_sampling} show that top-$p$ sampling mitigates some degeneration, but our results demonstrate that diversity still declines monotonically across recursive application of the same sampling procedure.\n\n\\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}\n\\label{sec:related-provenance}\n\nScalable data curation relies on quality signals to filter web-crawled corpora.\n\\citet{dsir} select pretraining data to match a target distribution, \\citet{qurating} learn quality scores from LLM judgments, and \\citet{ccnet} filter Common Crawl via perplexity-based heuristics.\nGeneration depth is a complementary provenance signal: it characterizes \\emph{how many steps removed} text is from a human source, orthogonal to content quality per se.\n\nAn alternative approach is proactive provenance through watermarking \\cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark}, where identifiable patterns are embedded during generation.\nHowever, \\citet{chainwash} demonstrate that watermark strength decays across multi-step generation chains, motivating passive post-hoc methods that do not require generator cooperation.\nOur depth fingerprinting is such a method: it operates on text alone without assuming access to the generating model or an embedded watermark.\nAt the feature level, our reference-model NLL analysis connects to \\citet{min_k_percent}, who use the probability distribution's lower tail for membership inference; we analyze the full NLL distribution for a different purpose---ordinal depth estimation rather than training-set membership.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}).\n\n\\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}\n\\label{sec:results-classification}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.789, significantly outperforming all single-detector baselines ($p<0.001$; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nPer-generator held-out F1 ranges from 0.683 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.856 (Pythia-1B).\nPer-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\\,=\\,0.94, gen-2+\\,=\\,0.80, gen-1\\,=\\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}\nThe six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists at scales beyond the $\\leq$2.8B training generators.\nHowever, cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B held-out F1\\,=\\,0.683 (CI [0.668, 0.694]), compared to 0.76--0.86 for $\\leq$2.8B generators.\nText generated by larger models is more fluent, compressing feature margins and making depth distinctions harder.\nNotably, adding Qwen3-8B data \\emph{improves} classification on the original five generators (+1.1\\,pp to 0.811), indicating that cross-scale diversity benefits rather than dilutes the training signal.\nWe note that this gap may narrow with scale-matched training data; our current dataset contains $\\sim$1{,}000 samples per generator regardless of scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}\nLeave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$2.6\\,pp vs.\\ LOGO).\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nText length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\\rho| < 0.14$).\nLength-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Generator} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Held-out F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & [0.843, 0.863] \\\\\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & [0.793, 0.813] \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & [0.754, 0.776] \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & [0.813, 0.834] \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.805 & [0.796, 0.817] \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & [0.668, 0.694] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & --- \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.800 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Leave-one-generator-out macro-F1 with bootstrap 95\\% confidence intervals ($n$\\,=\\,1000) for 3-class depth estimation using the 7-feature ensemble. Each row holds out one generator for testing and trains on the remaining five.}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\nFeatures exhibit \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.\nThis pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nIndividual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.475$),\nwhile shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.089$).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\\_ratio; F1\\,=\\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\\_decay; F1\\,=\\,0.745; McNemar $p \\approx 0$).\nPermutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).\nSurface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nTo confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we compute features using three cross-family references: Pythia-410M, OLMo-2-1B, and Qwen3-0.6B.\nAll three achieve LOGO F1 between 0.80 and 0.82, with consistent drift directions across features.\nThe pattern generalizes across reference model architectures.\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{LOGO F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\\_ratio (0.269) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\\_ratio (0.320) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\\_mean (0.218) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Single-detector CIs span 18--26\\,pp; the ensemble CI spans $<$\\,1\\,pp.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same ordinal classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation (Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{LOGO F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n7-feature ensemble & \\textbf{0.800} & [0.786, 0.800] \\\\\nAvg-LogProb        & 0.765 & [0.616, 0.820] \\\\\nLD-Score            & 0.721 & [0.588, 0.770] \\\\\nFastDetectGPT       & 0.627 & [0.511, 0.762] \\\\\nBinoculars          & 0.579 & [0.405, 0.664] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ordinal depth classification: 7-feature ensemble vs.\\ single detection baselines (5-gen LOGO). The ensemble outperforms the best single detector (Avg-LogProb) by +3.5\\,pp (paired bootstrap $p < 0.001$, $\\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ensemble outperforms all baselines in mean F1: +3.5\\,pp over Avg-LogProb ($p < 0.001$, $\\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]), +7.9\\,pp over LD-Score, +17.3\\,pp over FastDetectGPT, and +22.1\\,pp over Binoculars (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\nThe critical distinction is \\emph{reliability}.\nThe ensemble CI spans just 0.9\\,pp [0.783, 0.792], while single-detector CIs span 18--26\\,pp (e.g., Avg-LogProb [0.616, 0.820]).\nA single detector's CI is wider than \\emph{all pairwise detector differences}, meaning no single detector is reliably best across generators.\nEach detector captures partial depth signal---Avg-LogProb tracks mean perplexity shift, LD-Score captures character-level distributional change---but each fails on specific generator families.\nThe 7-feature ensemble aggregates complementary signals, producing stable performance regardless of the held-out generator.\nAcross all six generators including Qwen3-8B, the ensemble achieves F1\\,=\\,0.789 with a 95\\% CI of [0.783, 0.792] (width 0.9\\,pp).\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility: A Negative Finding}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nCan depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?\nWe fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\\ref{tab:downstream}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{$n$} & \\textbf{Test PPL} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAll depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nSize-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\nBinary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.\nTraining on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \\emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).\nCross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.\n\nRestricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\\,=\\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.\n\nWe report this negative finding transparently: depth fingerprinting is an effective \\emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \\emph{curative} tool for data curation.\nKnowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.\n",

The ensemble outperforms the best single detector (Avg-LogProb) by +3.5\\,pp (paired bootstrap $p < 0.001$, $\\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ensemble outperforms all baselines in mean F1: +3.5\\,pp over Avg-LogProb ($p < 0.001$, $\\Delta$ CI [3.0, 4.0]), +7.9\\,pp over LD-Score, +17.3\\,pp over FastDetectGPT, and +22.1\\,pp over Binoculars (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\nThe critical distinction is \\emph{reliability}.\nThe ensemble CI spans just 0.9\\,pp [0.783, 0.792], while single-detector CIs span 18--26\\,pp (e.g., Avg-LogProb [0.616, 0.820]).\nA single detector's CI is wider than \\emph{all pairwise detector differences}, meaning no single detector is reliably best across generators.\nEach detector captures partial depth signal---Avg-LogProb tracks mean perplexity shift, LD-Score captures character-level distributional change---but each fails on specific generator families.\nThe 7-feature ensemble aggregates complementary signals, producing stable performance regardless of the held-out generator.\nAcross all six generators including Qwen3-8B, the ensemble achieves F1\\,=\\,0.789 with a 95\\% CI of [0.783, 0.792] (width 0.9\\,pp).\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility: A Negative Finding}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nCan depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?\nWe fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\\ref{tab:downstream}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{$n$} & \\textbf{Test PPL} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAll depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nSize-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\nBinary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.\nTraining on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \\emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).\nCross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.\n\nRestricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\\,=\\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.\n\nWe report this negative finding transparently: depth fingerprinting is an effective \\emph{diagnostic} tool for characterizing text provenance, not a \\emph{curative} tool for data curation.\nKnowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " \\label{tab:baselines}",
          " \\end{table}",
          "+\\footnotetext{Bootstrap CIs are computed by resampling test-set predictions 1{,}000 times; the point estimate may coincide with a CI bound when the bootstrap distribution is skewed.}",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).\nDepth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale classifier transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\\,=\\,0.683 vs.\\ 0.76--0.86 for $\\leq$2.8B generators).\nFrontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.\n\nWe evaluate three prompt types (continuation, elaboration, rewriting; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}) but all share the same Pythia-2.8B generator.\nCross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753) confirms the depth signal generalizes, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.\n\nChains are constructed with a single generator model per step.\nReal-world web contamination likely involves heterogeneous generators at each depth, which could attenuate or alter the depth signal we observe.\nWe also do not evaluate robustness to human editing or post-processing of generated text, which is common in practice and may disrupt distributional features.\n\nThe reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) suggest the phenomenon is not reference-specific.\nAll seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.\n\nThe negative downstream result (\\S\\ref{sec:results-downstream}) is evaluated on causal language modeling only.\nOther downstream tasks (classification, summarization, instruction following) may respond differently to depth composition.\n\nWe use 3-class granularity (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.\n",
